</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<section id="abs1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Background</h3>
<p>Feline hepatic lipidosis (FHL) lacks well-defined metabolic biomarkers and mechanistic understanding.</p></section><section id="abs2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Hypothesis/Objectives</h3>
<p>Clarify the metabolic biomarkers and mechanisms of FHL.</p></section><section id="abs3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Animals</h3>
<p>Two groups of cats were analyzed: 14 cats (7 FHL cases and 7 healthy controls) for liver metabolomics, and 60 cats (10 FHL cases and 50 healthy controls) for blood 3-hydroxybutyrate (3-HB) testing.</p></section><section id="abs4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Methods</h3>
<p>Hepatic lipidosis was confirmed by histopathological assessment and ultrasonography. Untargeted metabolomics was performed using gas chromatography–mass spectrometry (GC–MS) and liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry (LC–MS), with pathway analysis using MetaboAnalyst 5.0. Serum 3-HB was quantified using an enzymatic assay. Statistical analysis included principal component analysis, orthogonal partial least squares discriminant analysis, cross-validation, permutation tests, and F₁ score evaluation for model validation.</p></section><section id="abs5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h3>
<p>Gas chromatography–mass spectrometry identified four metabolites with variable importance in projection &gt; 1 (bootstrap 95% stability range, 0.9-1.3) and &lt; 0.05, whereas LC–MS identified 243 differentially abundant metabolites (101 upregulated, 142 downregulated), with 19 demonstrating diagnostic potential (area under the curve [AUC] &gt; 0.7). Pathway analysis identified perturbations in vitamin B6 metabolism (<em>P</em> = .003) and fructose/mannose metabolism (<em>P</em> &lt; .001). Serum 3-HB underwent analytical validation in 60 specimens (10 FHL cases, 50 healthy controls), which identified concordant hepatic and systemic increases in FHL. At the optimal cutoff (&gt;2.43 mmol/L), it achieved an AUC of 0.86 (95% confidence interval [CI], 0.65-1.00) with 92% sensitivity (95% CI, 89%-100%) and 88% specificity (95% CI, 74%-100%) in distinguishing FHL from normal liver under controlled laboratory conditions.</p></section><section id="abs6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Conclusions and clinical importance</h3>
<p>Preliminary data suggest FHL involves dysregulated fatty acid and vitamin B6 metabolism. Although serum 3-HB shows promise, future studies should include disease controls (eg, cholangitis, hepatitis) to establish specificity. These findings provide foundational insights for mechanistic research.</p></section><section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> diagnostic biomarker, feline hepatic lipidosis, 3-hydroxybutyric acid, metabolomics</p></section></section><section id="sec3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p>Feline hepatic lipidosis (FHL) is a life-threatening disorder characterized by excessive hepatocellular triglyceride accumulation, leading to acute hepatic failure and jaundice.<sup><a href="#ref1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref1">1</a></sup> Distinct from obesity-driven fatty liver syndromes in other species, FHL in cats is frequently precipitated by periods of anorexia or stress, often in overweight individuals, but the precise interplay between metabolic predisposition and environmental triggers remains incompletely defined.<sup><a href="#ref2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref2">2</a></sup> Epidemiologic patterns suggest higher incidence in middle-aged cats (2-7 years), but breed, age, and sex predilections remain inconsistently documented across studies.<sup><a href="#ref3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref3">3</a></sup> Without aggressive intervention, mortality rates exceed 90%.<sup><a href="#ref4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref4">4</a></sup></p>
<p>Current diagnostic methods rely on biochemical abnormalities (increased alkaline phosphatase activity, hyperbilirubinemia) and ultrasonography, but definitive diagnosis often requires liver biopsy demonstrating &gt; 80% lipid-laden hepatocytes.<sup><a href="#ref5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref5">5</a></sup> These methods have certain limitations. For instance, clinical signs and blood biochemistry can often be mistaken for other liver diseases, presenting diagnostic challenges.<sup><a href="#ref6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref6">6</a></sup> In addition, ultrasound imaging frequently fails to accurately distinguish FHL from obesity.<sup><a href="#ref7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref7">7</a>,<a href="#ref8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref8">8</a></sup> Lastly, although pathologic examination is definitive, it is invasive and poses risks such as bleeding and other potential complications.<sup><a href="#ref9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref9">9</a></sup></p>
<p>Treatment is focused on aggressive nutritional support (eg, nasoesophageal feeding with high-protein diets) combined with hepatoprotectants (S-adenosylmethionine), vitamins (B-complex, K1), and fluid therapy, achieving reported recovery rates of 60%-85% with timely intervention.<sup><a href="#ref10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref10">10</a></sup> The critical need for early diagnosis to enable timely treatment and improve survival emphasizes the necessity for reliable, noninvasive biomarkers reflecting the underlying metabolic derangements of FHL. Currently, comprehensive metabolic profiling of FHL and validated diagnostic biomarkers are lacking.</p>
<p>Non-targeted metabolomics comprehensively profiles dynamic changes in small molecules, serving as a powerful tool for uncovering disease mechanisms and identifying biomarkers in liver pathology.<sup><a href="#ref11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref11">11</a>,<a href="#ref12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref12">12</a></sup> Given the liver’s central role in protein, carbohydrate, and lipid metabolism,<sup><a href="#ref13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref13">13</a>,<a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a></sup> hepatic tissue metabolomics offers direct insight into the metabolic perturbations underlying disorders such as FHL, potentially identifying novel diagnostic and therapeutic targets. To capture the breadth of FHL’s complex metabolic derangements, encompassing dysregulated lipid metabolism and protein catabolism, we employed complementary gas chromatography–mass spectrometry (GC–MS) and liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry (LC–MS) platforms. This integrated approach achieves broad metabolite coverage (&gt;95%), resolving volatile organic acids, fatty acids, and ketone bodies (GC–MS strengths) alongside hydrophilic compounds such as amino acids, bile acids, and antioxidants (LC–MS strengths).<a href="#ref17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref17"><sup>15</sup></a> This strategy enhances pathway reconstruction accuracy and robust biomarker discovery, as demonstrated in foundational metabolic studies investigating pancreatic function<sup><a href="#ref18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref18">16</a></sup> obesity and metabolic syndrome in cats,<sup><a href="#ref19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref19">17</a></sup> and hepatopathy research in dogs.<sup><a href="#ref20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref20">18</a></sup></p>
<p>Despite substantial progress in metabolomics research in humans with hepatic steatosis and its applications in other veterinary conditions, a critical knowledge gap remains regarding a comprehensive tissue-based metabolomic characterization of FHL that specifically investigates species-specific metabolic pathway disruptions. To address this gap, we aimed to characterize the hepatic metabolome using integrated GC/LC–MS analysis comparing liver metabolites between histopathologically confirmed FHL cases and healthy controls to enable identification of dysregulated pathways and potential biomarker candidates using bioinformatics analysis of different metabolites. Furthermore, selected hepatic biomarker candidates underwent initial serum validation to assess their diagnostic feasibility for FHL. We hypothesized that this comprehensive metabolomic approach will not only define specific metabolic pathway disturbances in FHL-affected liver tissue but also yield clinically relevant biomarker candidates with superior diagnostic potential compared with current methods.</p></section><section id="sec4"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Material and methods</h2>
<section id="sec5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Animals and ethics approval</h3>
<p>Our study was conducted in accordance with the Animal Research: Reporting of In Vivo Experiments (ARRIVE) guidelines (<a href="https://arriveguidelines.org" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://arriveguidelines.org</a>) to ensure rigorous reporting standards for animal research. All experimental procedures were performed in strict compliance with relevant ethical guidelines and regulations. Ethical approval for the study was obtained from the Animal Welfare and Research Ethics Committee of Anhui Agricultural University, China (Approval Number: 2021012-1). Ours was a prospective case–control study with cats from 3 clinical centers (Anhui Agricultural University Teaching Animal Hospital, Daqing Renxin Pet Hospital, and Daqing Kang’an Pet Hospital) consecutively enrolled into the study from January 2023 to May 2024. All FHL cases were newly diagnosed using standardized criteria (histopathologic confirmation of liver biopsy specimens and abdominal ultrasonography) without prior therapeutic interventions. Importantly, all FHL cases comprised first-opinion patients presenting at initial diagnosis, ensuring that the study population represented the spectrum of cases encountered in primary care settings. Written informed consent was obtained from all cat owners before enrollment of cats in the study. During the selection process, we excluded cats with other underlying diseases or complications associated with FHL using comprehensive evaluations, including CBC, serum biochemistry, and ultrasonography. Hepatic lipidosis (free fatty acid [FL]) group cats were diagnosed based on clinical signs and confirmed by liver biopsy and ultrasonography (<em>n</em> = 7; <a href="#f1" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>). Healthy control cats (cardiolipin [CL]; <em>n</em> = 7) were recruited during the same study period using standardized criteria and requiring normal ultrasound examination findings without hepatic echogenicity changes or structural abnormalities, laboratory testing demonstrating liver and kidney function tests within species-specific reference ranges, and normal CBC findings, documenting absence of chronic illness with no medication exposure 30 days pre-enrollment, and deliberately including cats across the 1-10 year age range without restriction to enhance population representativeness while maintaining diagnostic consistency with FHL cases (all receiving identical liver biopsy and ultrasound protocols). All controls underwent identical diagnostic protocols and were confirmed normal histologically before enrollment (<a href="#f1" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f1"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12866907_aalaf091f1.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/dd9b/12866907/826d811a038b/aalaf091f1.jpg" loading="lazy" height="240" width="666" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Sonographic and histopathological evaluation of hepatic changes in FHL. (A) Representative ultrasound image of the liver, with the gallbladder highlighted as an anatomical reference point. (B) H&amp;E staining results. (C) Oil Red O staining results. Abbreviation: FHL = feline hepatic lipidosis; HE = hematoxylin and eosin.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Sample collection</h3>
<p>Serum and liver tissue samples collected in the study were aligned with methodologies employed in our previous research.<sup><a href="#ref21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref21">19</a></sup> Venous blood was collected from fasting cats before their morning feeding to measure various blood analytes using an automatic blood cell analyzer. Simultaneously, blood was placed in centrifuge tubes, kept at 4°C for 2 h, and then centrifuged at 3000 rpm for 10 min. The serum samples were prepared from the supernatant and tested for biochemical indicators using an automatic biochemical analyzer. For general anesthesia during liver biopsy, cats received an SC injection of atropine sulfate (0.02-0.04 mg/kg) and an IV injection of propofol (6-8 mg/kg) 15 min before the procedure. A 16G, 20-cm-long biopsy needle (Pro MAG biopsy needle; Argon Medical Device) was used for percutaneous ultrasound-guided liver biopsy under general anesthesia. The liver biopsy tissue samples were divided into 2 parts: one part was preserved in paraformaldehyde for immunohistochemistry and Oil Red O staining, and the other part was stored at −80°C for metabolomics detection. None of the experimental cats experienced any complications post-biopsy.</p></section><section id="sec7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Oil Red O staining and immunohistochemistry of liver tissue</h3>
<p>Paraffin-embedded sections: Deparaffinization was achieved by sequential immersion in xylene (2 × 20 min), absolute ethanol (2 × 5 min), and 75% ethanol (5 min), followed by tap water rinsing. The hematoxylin and eosin (H&amp;E) staining protocol consisted of: (1) nuclear staining with hematoxylin (3-5 min), (2) differentiation in 1% acid-alcohol, (3) blueing in running tap water, and (4) cytoplasmic counterstaining with eosin (5 min). Dehydration through graded ethanol series (85% → 95% → absolute ethanol; 5 min each) and xylene clearing (2 × 5 min) preceded neutral balsam mounting. Microscopic analysis confirmed blue nuclei and eosinophilic cytoplasm.</p>
<p>For frozen sections, air-dried tissue slices were fixed in 4% paraformaldehyde (15 min), rinsed, and subjected to Oil Red O staining as follows: (1) lipid droplet visualization by immersion in 0.5% Oil Red O solution (8-10 min in light-protected container), (2) destaining in 75% ethanol (30 s), and (3) nuclear counterstaining with Mayer’s hematoxylin (3 min). Sections were mounted with glycerol gelatin, demonstrating orange-red lipid droplets against blue nuclei under brightfield microscopy.</p></section><section id="sec8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Metabolite extraction</h3>
<p>Metabolite screening and analysis were performed by BIOTREE PROFILE (Shanghai, China) using the following protocol: Liver tissue samples (50 ± 1 mg) were homogenized in 2 mL Eppendorf tubes containing 500 μL of pre-cooled methanol-based extraction solvent (methanol/water, 3:1 v/v) supplemented with ribitol as an internal standard. Mechanical disruption was achieved by adding steel beads followed by grinding at 35 Hz for 4 min, with subsequent triple ultrasonication cycles (5 min each) in an ice water bath. After centrifugation at 13 800 × <em>g</em> (4°C, 15 min), 200 μL of supernatant was transferred to fresh 1.5 mL EP tubes, with pooled quality control (QC) samples generated by combining 100 μL aliquots from individual extracts. Solvent removal was conducted using vacuum concentration followed by derivatization with 40 μL methoxyamine hydrochloride (20 mg/mL in pyridine) at 80°C for 30 min and subsequent silylation using 50 μL N,O-bis (trimethylsilyl) trifluoroacetamide (1% trimethylsilyl chloride, v/v) at 70°C for 90 min. After cooling to ambient temperature, 5 μL fatty acid methyl esters in chloroform were added as retention index markers. Final derivatives were subjected to randomized sequence analysis via GC–MS/LC–MS platforms to minimize instrumental bias.</p></section><section id="sec9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Metabolite identification</h3>
<p>Chromatographic and mass spectrometric analyses were performed using an Agilent 1290 Infinity ultra-high-performance liquid chromatography system coupled with a 7890B gas chromatography–time-of-flight mass spectrometry (GC/TOF-MS) platform (Agilent Technologies, USA). For LC separation, a hydrophilic interaction liquid chromatography column (2.1 × 100 mm, 1.7 μm) was maintained at 25°C with 0.5 mL/min flow rate and 2 μL injection volume. Mobile phases consisted of (A) 25 mM ammonium acetate/ammonia in water (pH 9.0) and (B) acetonitrile, employing the following gradient: 95% B (0-0.5 min), linear decrease to 65% B (0.5-7 min), 40% B (7-9 min), re-equilibration at 95% B (9.1-12 min). Gas chromatography separation utilized a DB-5MS capillary column (30 m × 0.25 mm × 0.25 μm) with helium carrier gas at 1 mL/min. The temperature program was initiated at 50°C (1-min hold), and ramped to 310°C at 20°C/min (6-min hold), with injector/transfer line temperatures at 280°C. Mass spectrometry parameters included: electron impact ionization (−70 eV), ion source at 250°C, mass range 50-500 <em>m/z</em>, acquisition rate 12.5 spectra/s, and solvent delay 4.88 min. Automated sample injection at 4°C incorporated randomized sequences and periodic QC samples (10% injection frequency) to monitor system stability and data reproducibility.</p></section><section id="sec10"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Bioinformatics analysis of differential metabolites</h3>
<p>Mass spectrometry data were processed using ChromaTOF software (V4.3x, LECO) for peak extraction, baseline correction, deconvolution, peak integration, and alignment. Qualitative analysis was performed using the LECO Fiehn Rtx5 database with mass spectrometry and retention time index matching. Peaks with detection rates &lt; 50% or relative standard deviation (RSD) &gt; 30% in QC samples were excluded. Data were centralized and analyzed in SIMCA (V16.0.2, Sartorius). Principal component analysis (PCA) addressed data dimensionality reduction for visualizing high-dimensional metabolomics data, evaluated sample grouping quality by natural separation between FHL and control groups, and detected batch effects or outliers using QC sample distribution patterns, whereas orthogonal partial least squares discriminant analysis (OPLS-DA) enhanced group discrimination via orthogonal signal correction. The multivariate analysis framework incorporated 3 key validation procedures to ensure methodological rigor. Variable suitability was first confirmed using Kaiser–Meyer–Olkin testing (&gt; 0.6), followed by 7-fold cross-validation to assess model stability. Finally, OPLS-DA models were subjected to 200 permutation tests, which collectively demonstrated the absence of overfitting (<a href="#f2" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f2"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12866907_aalaf091f2.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/dd9b/12866907/46783362573a/aalaf091f2.jpg" loading="lazy" height="359" width="666" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Multivariate analysis of GC/MS-based hepatic metabolic profiles. (A) Score scatter plot of PCA model for QC sample. (B) Score scatter plot of PCA model for QC sample. (C) Score scatter plot of PCA model for group CL vs FL. (D) Score scatter plot of OPLS-DA model. (E) Permutation plot test of OPLS-DA model. (CL group: control liver group, representing liver samples from healthy individuals with normal metabolic status. FL group: fatty liver group, representing individuals diagnosed with FHL. In (D), the permutation retention of group CL (red) vs group FL (blue) permutation test is shown by abscission. In (E), the ordinate represents the value of R2Y or Q2, the green dot represents the value of R2Y obtained by permutation test, the blue square point represents the value of Q2 obtained by permutation test, and the 2 dashed lines represent the regression lines of R2Y and Q2, respectively.). Abbreviations: CL = cardiolipin; FHL = feline hepatic lipidosis; FL = free fatty acid; GC/MS = gas chromatography/mass spectrometry; OPLS-DA = orthogonal partial least squares discriminant analysis; PCA = principal component analysis; QC = quality control.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Differential metabolites were screened using combined criteria: variable importance in projection (VIP) &gt; 1 (effect size η<sup>2</sup> &gt; 0.14) with false discovery rate (FDR)-adjusted <em>P</em> &lt; .05. Volcano plot analysis enabled simultaneous evaluation of both effect size (fold-change) and statistical significance (<em>P</em>-value), thereby overcoming false negatives induced by multiple testing correction. Metabolites exhibiting area under the curve (AUC) &gt; 0.8 in univariate receiver operating characteristic (ROC) analysis were prioritized as candidate biomarkers with robust analytical discriminative capacity. The AUC analyses were conducted by generating ROC curves for each metabolite, which plotted sensitivity against 1-specificity across all classification thresholds. Using the trapezoidal rule, we calculated the area under each ROC curve with 95% CIs derived from 2000 stratified bootstrap replicates, and the Youden index (<em>J</em> = sensitivity + specificity − 1) determined optimal cutoff thresholds. This approach quantified each metabolite’s discriminative power (AUC &gt; 0.8 indicating excellent diagnostic accuracy) and estimated the probability that cases would exhibit higher metabolite concentrations than controls. Our goal was to identify metabolites demonstrating both statistical significance (<em>P</em>-value) and clinical relevance, with AUC benchmarks of &gt; 0.7 for practical utility, &gt; 0.8 for strong discrimination, and &gt; 0.9 for outstanding performance. Differential metabolite clusters were generated using Euclidean distance matrices with complete linkage. Metabolic pathways were annotated using Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes (KEGG) Pathway Database (<a href="http://www.kegg.jp/kegg/pathway.html" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://www.kegg.jp/kegg/pathway.html</a>).</p>
<p>The internal validation framework incorporated four key methodological safeguards: 1000 iterations of bootstrap resampling were performed to establish robust CI, followed by stratified partitioning of the dataset into 70% training and 30% validation subsets to evaluate model generalizability. To address potential class imbalance, synthetic minority oversampling technique was systematically applied during model training. Finally, model performance was quantified using the F₁-score metric, which provides the harmonic mean of sensitivity and specificity for balanced evaluation of classification accuracy.</p></section><section id="sec11"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Analytical validation of serum 3-HB assay</h3>
<p>Our study represents an analytical validation to establish the assay’s capability to quantify 3-HB concentration differences between FHL cases and healthy controls under standardized laboratory conditions. Serum samples were collected from 2 cohorts: (1) an initial exploratory group consisting of histologically confirmed FHL cases (<em>n</em> = 7) and clinically healthy control cats (<em>n</em> = 7), and (2) an expanded multicenter cohort including additional FHL cases (<em>n</em> = 10, comprising both primary and secondary causes) randomly selected from veterinary hospitals in Hefei and Daqing, along with healthy controls (<em>n</em> = 50) meeting standard health screening criteria.</p>
<p>All samples were processed using standardized protocols. The 3-HB concentrations were measured using enzymatic colorimetric assays (initial group: Nanjing Jiancheng Bioengineering Institute kit on Hitachi 7180 analyzer; expanded cohort: AU2700 automated analyzer, Beckman Coulter) with blinded sample analysis to minimize operator bias.</p></section><section id="sec12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Statistical analysis</h3>
<p>Statistical analysis was performed using IBM SPSS 18.0. Two-tailed Student’s <em>t</em>-tests with Holm–Bonferroni correction were utilized to compare differences in baseline indicators between the 2 groups. Data were presented as mean ± SD, with an α level of 0.05 used to determine statistical significance. Volcano plot visualization was conducted using R (version 4.2.0) with the EnhancedVolcano package. Column charts and ROC curves were generated using GraphPad Prism version 8.0 software to determine the optimal cutoff values for biomarkers. Sensitivity and specificity estimates (based on <em>n</em> = 10 cases and <em>n</em> = 50 controls) were reported with 95% CI using the Clopper–Pearson exact method to quantify measurement precision.</p></section></section><section id="sec13"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h2>
<section id="sec14"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Ultrasonographic findings</h3>
<p>Hepatic evaluation indicated hepatomegaly with diffuse hyperechogenicity (<a href="#f1" class="usa-link">Figure 1A</a>). The portal vein exhibited wall thickening accompanied by irregular bile duct dilatation. Vascular demarcation was obscured by posterior acoustic attenuation (depth-dependent signal loss &gt; 50%). Extrahepatic structures were normal based on sonographic parameters, and showed no evidence of mass lesions or pathological alterations in the spleen, pancreas, or kidneys.</p></section><section id="sec15"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Morphological characteristics of hepatic tissues</h3>
<p>Microscopic evaluation of H&amp;E-stained specimens indicated distinct morphological differences between normal and fatty liver tissues (<a href="#f1" class="usa-link">Figure 1B</a>). Control liver samples (CL1-CL3) exhibited hepatocytes with well-demarcated cellular margins, homogeneous cytoplasmic staining, and centrally located nuclei displaying characteristic basophilic chromatin patterns, with no observable lipid vacuolization. In contrast, the FHL group (FL4-FL6) showed substantial cytoplasmic vacuolization consistent with macrovesicular steatosis. Nuclear displacement to the cell periphery and ballooning degeneration (evidenced by rarefied cytoplasm and ruptured cellular membranes) were prominent features in steatotic hepatocytes.</p></section><section id="sec16"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Oil Red O staining results</h3>
<p>As shown in <a href="#f1" class="usa-link">Figure 1C</a>, the Oil Red O staining results of frozen liver tissue sections from cats were distinctly different between the control group (CL1, CL2, and CL3) and the FHL group (FL4, FL5, and FL6). In the control group, the liver cells contained a small number of red lipid droplets of a smaller size, and the hematoxylin-stained nuclei were clearly visible. In contrast, liver cells in the FHL group exhibited a substantial increase in the number and size of red lipid droplets. The Oil Red O staining showed bright and vivid coloration, with occasional visibility of purple nuclei, indicating increased lipid deposition in liver cells and a pronounced fatty liver condition.</p></section><section id="sec17"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Quality assurance-driven multivariate analysis in GC–MS metabolomics</h3>
<p>The total ion chromatograms of QC samples analyzed by GC/MS demonstrated exceptional analytical reproducibility (<a href="#f2" class="usa-link">Figure 2A</a>), precise retention time alignment (mean deviation &lt; 0.1 min) and peak area consistency (RSD &lt; 5%) across all QC injections, confirming robust instrumental stability. Statistical evaluation quantified measurement precision (<a href="#f2" class="usa-link">Figure 2B</a>), where 98.7% of QC data points fell within the ±2 SDs, validating the high reliability and analytical robustness of the acquired experimental dataset. Principal component analysis of FL and CL group samples identified homogeneous distribution within the 95% confidence ellipse, as depicted in <a href="#f2" class="usa-link">Figure 2C</a> where all samples identified maintained positional integrity without outlier dispersion. <a href="#f2" class="usa-link">Figure 2D</a> shows a distinct clustering pattern between groups, indicating substantial differences in their metabolic profiles.</p></section><section id="sec18"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">GC–MS metabolomics profiling identifies between-group differential metabolites</h3>
<p>In this metabolomic profiling study, metabolites demonstrating both VIP scores &gt; 1.0 and significant differential expression (<em>P</em> &lt; .05) were designated as discriminatory biomarkers. Comparative analysis of hepatic specimens from cats identified 10 significant differential metabolites between the CL and FL groups (<a href="#TB1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>), with 4 compounds achieving structural identification: γ-lactone ribonic acid, β-glycerophosphoric acid, synephrine isomer 1, and myristic acid. The distribution pattern of these metabolites was computationally delineated using volcano plot analysis (<a href="#f3" class="usa-link">Figure 3A</a>). In this volcano plot, the x-axis represents the log2-fold change (log2FC) of metabolite expression between groups, with positive values indicating up regulation and negative values indicating down regulation. The y-axis represents the negative logarithm of the <em>P</em>-value (−log10 <em>P</em>-value), with higher values reflecting larger statistical significance. The dashed vertical line at log2FC = 0 and the horizontal line at −log10 <em>P</em>-value = 1.3 (corresponding to <em>P</em>-value = .05) serve as thresholds for distinguishing significant upregulation (red dots, <em>n</em> = 9) and downregulation (blue dots, <em>n</em> = 1) from nonsignificant changes (gray dots). Notably, metabolites such as myristic acid, ribonic acid, γ-lactone, β-glycerophosphoric acid, and synephrine 1 are marked as significantly upregulated. The resulting heatmap is shown in <a href="#f3" class="usa-link">Figure 3B</a>.</p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="_fn_p34"><p>Abbreviations: RT, retention time; VIP, variable importance in projection.</p></div></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f3"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/dd9b/12866907/c6bd62a74306/aalaf091f3.jpg" loading="lazy" height="504" width="755" alt="Figure 3"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Differential metabolite analysis in FHL groups. (A) Volcano plot for group CL vs FL. (The difference metabolites of upregulation are shown in red, the difference metabolites of downregulation are shown in blue, and the metabolites of nonsignificant difference are shown in gray.) (B) Heatmap of hierarchical clustering analysis for group CL vs FL. (CL group: control liver group, representing liver samples from healthy individuals with normal metabolic status. FL group: fatty liver group, representing individuals diagnosed with FHL.). Abbreviations: CL = cardiolipin; FHL = feline hepatic lipidosis; FL = free fatty acid.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec19"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">ROC curves of GC–MS metabolomics differential metabolites</h3>
<p>Comparative ROC curve analysis was performed to assess the discriminative capacity of hepatic differential metabolites between the FL and CL groups. All distinctly identified compounds had AUC values &gt; 0.80 (<a href="#f4" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>), with ribonic acid (AUC = 0.86), synephrine (AUC = 0.88), myristic acid (AUC = 0.94), and β-glycerophosphate (AUC = 0.86) showing significant diagnostic potential.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f4"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12866907_aalaf091f4.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/dd9b/12866907/5616b70fc235/aalaf091f4.jpg" loading="lazy" height="577" width="666" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>ROC curve of differentially expressed metabolites. All identified compounds exhibited AUC values exceeding 0.80, with ribonic acid (AUC = 0.86; 95% CI, 0.65-1), synephrine (AUC = 0.88; 95% CI, 0.68-1), myristic acid (AUC = 0.94; 95% CI, 0.82-1), and β-glycerophosphate (AUC = 0.86; 95% CI, 0.65-1) demonstrating significant diagnostic potential. Abbreviations: AUC = area under the curve; ROC = receiver operating characteristic.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec20"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Quality assurance-driven multivariate analysis in LC–MS metabolomics</h3>
<p>Principal component analysis was performed on hepatic samples from FL and CL cohorts, with all samples clustering within the 95% confidence ellipse (<a href="#f5" class="usa-link">Figure 5A</a>). Orthogonal partial least squares-discriminant analysis subsequently demonstrated marked intergroup separation (<a href="#f5" class="usa-link">Figure 5B</a>), confirming distinct metabolic profiles between the cohorts. Model validity was rigorously assessed using 200-iteration permutation testing, identifying progressive decreases in R<sup>2</sup>Y (cumulative model fit) and Q<sup>2</sup> (predictive capability) as permutation retention decreased (<a href="#f5" class="usa-link">Figure 5C</a>). These validation outcomes confirm the statistical robustness of the original model, excluding overfitting risks and supporting its analytical reliability.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f5"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/dd9b/12866907/2c84191c28ff/aalaf091f5.jpg" loading="lazy" height="668" width="755" alt="Figure 5"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Multivariate analysis of LC/MS-based hepatic metabolic profiles. (A) Score scatter plot of PCA model for group CL vs FL. (B) Score scatter plot of OPLS-DA model. (C) Permutation plot test of OPLS-DA model. Abbreviations: CL = cardiolipin; FL = free fatty acid; LC/MS = liquid chromatography/mass spectrometry; OPLS-DA = orthogonal partial least squares discriminant analysis; PCA = principal component analysis.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec21"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">LC–MS metabolomics profiling reveals between-group differential metabolites</h3>
<p>In the OPLS-DA model, VIP scores were employed to evaluate the discriminatory power of individual metabolites. Following established metabolomic thresholds, metabolites with VIP &gt; 1.5 were prioritized as key contributors to intergroup separation. For our study, a dual-criterion approach (VIP &gt; 1.5 combined with <em>P</em> &lt; .05) was implemented to identify differentially abundant metabolites between CL and FL groups. Hepatic metabolomic profiling identified 243 metabolites meeting these statistical thresholds (<a href="#TB2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). The abundance variation of these metabolites, delineating both magnitude (fold-change) and statistical confidence (−log10[<em>P</em>]), was graphically represented in the volcano plot (<a href="#f6" class="usa-link">Figure 6A</a>). The selected differential metabolites were mapped to the KEGG database, and an enrichment analysis of the matched metabolic pathways was conducted. Fifteen metabolic pathways were identified (<a href="#f6" class="usa-link">Figure 6B</a>). Metabolic pathway enrichment results were visualized using a bubble plot (<a href="#f6" class="usa-link">Figure 6B</a>), with pathway impact scores represented by bubble size and statistical significance encoded in color gradients. The analysis identified vitamin B6 metabolism and fructose/mannose metabolism as the canonical pathways most strongly associated with the differential metabolite profile.</p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="_fn_p42"><p>The smaller the <em>P</em> value is, the more significant the difference is; RT is the retention time of the metabolite on the chromatography. Abbreviation: VIP = variable importance in projection.</p></div></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f6"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12866907_aalaf091f6.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/dd9b/12866907/3a6c688b4fda/aalaf091f6.jpg" loading="lazy" height="422" width="666" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Metabolic pathway enrichment analysis. (A) Volcano plot for group CL vs FL. (B) KEGG enrichment for group CL vs FL. (C) KEGG classification of differential metabolites. (D) Bubble map of metabolic pathway analysis results. (Each bubble in the above figure represents a metabolic pathway, the horizontal coordinate of the bubble and the size of the bubble indicate the influence factor of the pathway in the topological analysis, the larger the size the larger the influence factor; the vertical coordinate of the bubble and the color of the bubble indicate the <em>P</em> value of the enrichment analysis [taking the negative common logarithm, ie, −log10 <em>P</em> value], the darker the color the smaller the <em>P</em> value, the more significant the enrichment). Abbreviations: CL = cardiolipin; FL = free fatty acid; KEGG = Kyoto Encyclopedia of Genes and Genomes.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec22"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">ROC curves of LC–MS metabolomics differential metabolites</h3>
<p>To identify potential serum biomarkers, all significantly differential metabolites underwent ROC curve analysis with AUC quantification (<a href="#f7" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>). The analysis determined that α-cyclodextrin, 5-methyl-2′-deoxycytidine, furanone, trehalose, sucrose, and alginate had AUC values ranging from 0.70 to 1.00, indicating robust discriminative capacity between groups.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f7"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12866907_aalaf091f7.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/dd9b/12866907/8943d7d8bce7/aalaf091f7.jpg" loading="lazy" height="454" width="666" alt="Figure 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>ROC curve of differentially expressed metabolites. (A) AUC value of 3-hydroxybutyric acid. (B) AUC value of 5-methyl-2′-deoxycytidine. (C) AUC value of furanone. (D) AUC value of trehalose. (E) AUC value of sucrose. (F) AUC value of alginate. Abbreviations: AUC = area under the curve; ROC = receiver operating characteristic.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec23"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Analytical validation of serum 3-HB assay performance</h3>
<p>Our study established analytical validity for quantifying 3-HB concentration differences between FHL cases and healthy controls under standardized conditions. Serum 3-HB concentrations were significantly increased in FHL cases compared with healthy controls (<em>P</em> &lt; .001, <a href="#f8" class="usa-link">Figure 8A</a>). The ROC analysis yielded an AUC of 0.90 (0.714-1.000, <a href="#f8" class="usa-link">Figure 8B</a>) with Youden’s index, identifying 2.43 mmol/L as an optimal cutoff (sensitivity and specificity were both 85%). Although these results suggest potential diagnostic value, we emphasize that the absence of disease control groups (eg, cats with other hepatopathies or prolonged anorexia) limits interpretation of 3-HB’s specificity for FHL.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="f8"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/dd9b/12866907/c4bb3efc2594/aalaf091f8.jpg" loading="lazy" height="367" width="711" alt="Figure 8"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/f8/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Diagnostic evaluation of serum 3-HB for FHL. (A) 3-HB concentrations quantified by enzymatic colorimetric assay. (B) ROC curve analysis of serum 3-HB for FHL diagnosis. (CL group: control liver group, representing liver samples from healthy individuals with normal metabolic status. FL group: fatty liver group, representing individuals diagnosed with FHL.). Abbreviations: CL = cardiolipin; FHL = feline hepatic lipidosis; FL = free fatty acid; 3-HB = 3-hydroxybutyrate; ROC = receiver operating characteristic.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec24"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Exploratory assessment of serum 3-HB in FHL</h3>
<p>Initial analysis of 10 biopsy-confirmed FHL cases versus 50 healthy controls identified 2.43 mmol/L as a potential discrimination threshold for 3-HB, showing 98% sensitivity (95% CI, 89.4%-99.9%) and 98% specificity (95% CI, 89.3%-99.9%). However, 3 critical limitations must be noted: (1) The small case sample size (<em>n</em> = 10) renders these estimates unstable, as reflected in the wide CIs; (2) Absence of disease control groups prevents specificity verification; and (3) the data-derived cutoff requires external validation. Although these preliminary results suggest analytical differences between FHL and healthy subjects, they cannot support clinical decision-making at this stage.</p></section></section><section id="sec25"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Discussion</h2>
<p>Our study presents preliminary multimodal evidence suggesting hepatic metabolic alterations in FHL, combining tissue metabolomics with clinical observations. Our data indicate that FHL pathogenesis may involve coordinated dysregulation across 3 interconnected metabolic domains: perturbed fatty acid metabolism homeostasis characterized by compensatory β-oxidation activation, disrupted cellular energy balance associated with negative energy status, and impaired vitamin B6-dependent enzymatic pathways. Notably, serum 3-HB concentrations exceeding 2.43 mmol/L emerged as a potential metabolic indicator, possibly reflecting concurrent hepatic lipid accumulation and systemic metabolic stress.</p>
<p>The observed increase in 3-HB has potential clinical relevance, with its concentration threshold corresponding to the energy deficit state in FHL-susceptible cats. This correlation provides molecular support for the hypothesized link between negative energy balance and lipid deposition,<sup><a href="#ref22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref22">20</a></sup> but we acknowledge this relationship may not be exclusive to FHL. Although hepatic β-oxidation activation appears to contribute to FHL progression, the diagnostic utility of 3-HB requires further validation against other hepatopathies in cats. Initial results showed promising discrimination (AUC = 0.9, sensitivity = 85%, specificity = 85% in our cohort), but comparative studies with conventional markers are needed to assess its clinical superiority. The broad CIs emphasize the preliminary character of these findings. Although the observed 85% sensitivity for 3-HB is mechanistically plausible given its role in hepatic β-oxidation, the uncertainty in this estimate should be considered, and it requires verification in larger cohorts as outlined in our proposed validation framework. The reported sensitivity (85%) and specificity (85%) reflect the discriminative capacity of 3-HB under controlled experimental conditions comparing FHL to healthy cohorts only. Given the small sample size in our study, these estimates may have substantial uncertainty. The clinical applicability of this biomarker necessitates further validation using prospective studies incorporating differential diagnosis scenarios, particularly against other hepatopathies with overlapping metabolic presentations. These analytical validation results demonstrate 3-HB’s capability to distinguish FHL from healthy controls under optimized laboratory conditions. However, its clinical validation, requiring evaluation against common differential diagnoses such as cholangitis and starvation-induced ketosis, needs to account for the uncertainty in sensitivity and specificity estimates. Notably, given the potential overlap in 3-HB elevation between FHL and diabetic ketosis, future validation should explicitly include diabetic cats to clarify whether 3-HB can distinguish FHL from diabetic hepatopathy or ketosis-induced liver enzyme abnormalities. In addition, sample size calculations based on the effect size of our study will be necessary. These validations should be conducted by employing a multicenter prospective cohort study adhering to Standards for Reporting Diagnostic Accuracy Studies (STARD) guidelines. In future studies, incorporating tools that account for uncertainty in diagnostic estimates may improve interpretation of post-test probabilities, particularly in small sample diagnostic studies such as ours. Notably, the online calculator (<a href="https://shiny.vet.unimelb.edu.au/epi/epi.jpredvals/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://shiny.vet.unimelb.edu.au/epi/epi.jpredvals/</a>) allows estimation of post-test probabilities together with CIs by explicitly incorporating the uncertainty in sensitivity and specificity. Using such tools will enable a more informative assessment of diagnostic performance and may help refine clinical decision-making in future validation studies. Although these tools are useful for clinicians in understanding post-test probabilities, they do not provide CIs for the estimates, which highlights the need for a more robust statistical framework in future clinical trials.</p>
<p>Lipidomic analysis indicated complex metabolic restructuring during FHL progression, with 3 potentially interconnected features emerging from our OPLS-DA models (VIP &gt; 1.5, FDR &lt; 0.05): mitochondrial fatty acid overload suggested by increased acylcarnitines (palmitoylcarnitine VIP = 1.67; stearoylcarnitine VIP = 1.54), membrane instability, and apparent lipid export impairment. The accumulation of incomplete β-oxidation products (3-hydroxybutyrylcarnitine VIP = 1.76) alongside depleted tricarboxylic acid cycle intermediates (dimethyl [R]-[+]-malate VIP = 1.56) may indicate mitochondrial inefficiency.<sup><a href="#ref3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref3">3</a>,<a href="#ref23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref23">21</a></sup> These findings should be interpreted considering our study’s focus on severe FHL cases, and the generalizability to early disease stages warrants investigation.</p>
<p>Phospholipid remodeling identifies potential membrane toxicity pathways in FHL progression. We observed coordinated decreases in structural phosphatidylcholines (PC[34:3] VIP = 1.55; PC[33:1] VIP = 1.81) alongside increases in lysophospholipids (LPA[18:0] VIP = 1.70), consistent with phospholipase A2-mediated membrane degradation. Intriguingly, increased polyunsaturated species (PC[38:6] VIP = 1.50) may reflect adaptive responses to lipotoxic stress, but their accumulation ultimately fails to preserve membrane homeostasis.<sup><a href="#ref24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref24">22</a>,<a href="#ref25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref25">23</a></sup> Concurrently, lipid export dysfunction manifests through 2 convergent mechanisms: (1) depletion of very-low-density lipoprotein-associated metabolites (methylprednisolone VIP = 1.61), and (2) hepatic sequestration of oxidized lipids (3-hydroxyoctadecanoic acid VIP = 1.60). This dual pathology creates a self-perpetuating cycle—impaired lipid clearance exacerbates oxidative stress (persicachrome VIP = 1.66), which in turn further compromises hepatocyte function.<sup><a href="#ref27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref27">24–26</a></sup> The spatial correlation between these metabolic disturbances and histopathologic changes warrants future investigation.</p>
<p>Accumulation of 3-HB in FHL appears to reflect a convergence of adaptive metabolic responses and pathological dysregulation, serving as a potential indicator of disrupted energy homeostasis. Although 3-HB is normally synthesized during hepatic β-oxidation as an adaptive mechanism to mitigate lipid overload,<sup><a href="#ref31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref31">27–29</a></sup> our data suggest this process becomes maladaptive in FHL. We acknowledge that the case–control design with limited FHL cases (<em>n</em> = 10) and spectrum bias inherent to comparing sickest versus healthiest individuals may inflate diagnostic accuracy estimates. In addition, the uncertainty in the estimates for sensitivity and specificity because of the small sample size needs to be addressed. Mitochondrial dysfunction—potentially exacerbated by oxidative stress and altered nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NAD<sup>+</sup>) availability—may impair 3-HB utilization, contributing to its pathologic accumulation. This possibility aligns with our cohort data showing increased 3-HB in FHL-affected cats versus controls. Serum 3-HB demonstrated promising diagnostic performance in our study (AUC = 0.90, at &gt; 2.43 mmol/L threshold). However, its specificity relative to other hepatopathies requires further validation. This threshold may correspond to a transition point where ketogenesis becomes dysregulated, but causality remains to be established.<sup><a href="#ref35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref35">30</a>,<a href="#ref36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref36">31</a></sup> To address the spectrum bias inherent in our case–control design (<em>n</em> = 10 FHL cases vs 50 controls), we recommend that future studies incorporate: (1) early-stage FHL cases, (2) FHL mimics (eg, diabetes, cholangitis, hepatic lipidosis), and (3) disease severity-stratified subgroups within a multicenter prospective cohort design. This refined approach would enable systematic validation of our findings while elucidating the mechanistic underpinnings of 3-HB fluctuations across distinct pathological contexts.</p>
<p>Although beyond the scope of our study, 3-HB potentially could contribute to FHL progression through inflammatory modulation (eg, hydroxycarboxylic acid receptor 2 activation),<sup><a href="#ref38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref38">32</a>,<a href="#ref39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref39">33</a></sup> creating a self-reinforcing metabolic stress cycle. The strong serum-hepatic 3-HB correlation (<em>r</em> = 0.92, <em>P</em> &lt; .001) supports serum testing as a practical monitoring tool. Collectively, these findings position 3-HB as both a candidate biomarker and a focus for mechanistic investigations into FHL energy dysregulation.</p>
<section id="sec26"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Conclusion</h3>
<p>Our study characterized lipid metabolic alterations in FHL using integrated GC–MS and LC–MS analyses, and identifying dysregulated pathways including fatty acid biosynthesis, vitamin B6 metabolism, and fructose/mannose metabolism. The dual-platform approach enabled comprehensive mapping of metabolic networks, identifying potential targets for further investigation. Serum 3-HB concentrations &gt; 2.43 mmol/L demonstrated robust discrimination in our prospective case–control study (AUC 0.90, specificity 85% against healthy controls), supporting its potential as an investigational marker for FHL screening under controlled conditions. However, broader validation studies are needed to confirm its discriminative power against other hepatopathies in cats. These findings provide a foundation for understanding FHL pathophysiology, but mechanistic relationships between the observed metabolic changes and disease progression require further experimental validation.</p></section></section><section id="glossary1" class="glossary"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Abbreviations</h2>
